# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Or you can fork this repository and set your own key words in `main.py`:

Last update: 2026-10-19

👍Thanks to [zezhishao/DailyArXiv](https://github.com/zezhishao/DailyArXiv) and [Cool Paper](https://papers.cool).

| **[Evaluation Awareness Beyond English: Do LLMs Act Differently When Tested in Indic Languages?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22333v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22333v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can often tell when they are being tested. This ability, known as evaluation awareness, can lead models to act more cautiously during safety evaluations than in real use. Existing work has studied evaluation awareness almost entirely in English, although models serve users in many languages. Indic languages are spoken by over a billion people, yet evaluation awareness remains almost entirely unstudied in them. We test four instruction-tuned models from three families in five Indic languages alongside English. A set of cues varies whether the model is told that it is monitored and whether the monitoring is a safety test. Models refuse harmful requests more often when they are told that they are being tested. This increase appears in all four models and all six languages. In Indic languages, refusal of harmful requests rises by 14.4 percentage points on average. The test cue also brings Indic refusal of harmful requests level with English, but raises refusal of benign Indic requests from 6.9% to 11.3%. We release all 47,880 judged responses to support further work. These results suggest that safety evaluations in Indic languages may not reflect how models treat their everyday users.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning to Steer LLM Agents and Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22316v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22316v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-based agents and multi-agent systems increasingly tackle complex tasks through long-horizon tool use and inter-agent collaboration. However, these systems are prone to deviating from the overall goal as execution unfolds, which wastes execution resources and leads to undesired outcomes, a failure termed goal drift. In this work, we propose a steering agent that supervises execution on the fly and steers the system back on track when drift occurs. The steering agent observes the execution context, deciding when to intervene with a low-cost fast path and how to intervene with a high-cost slow path. To unlock its steering intelligence, the ability to assess goal drift and generate effective interventions, we jointly train the two paths with reinforcement learning, combining a pairwise ranking objective with group-relative policy optimization (GRPO). Experiments on information-seeking search tasks show that the steering agent provides effective supervision for both single-agent and multi-agent systems, improving their performance and efficiency. The learned steering intelligence generalizes to out-of-domain tasks, transfers to unseen executors, and supports controllable intervention frequency without retraining.</p></details> | 21 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Grid-Bench: Can LLMs Predict How Discrete Worlds Change?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22312v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22312v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial instructions describe how to move objects, transform regions, or modify their surroundings. When several operations are applied in sequence, each acts on a configuration shaped by earlier changes. How accurately can large language models (LLMs) predict the resulting spatial state? We introduce Grid-Bench, an executable benchmark in which eight families of operations transform discrete worlds represented as two-, three-, and four-dimensional grids. Given an initial grid, explicit rules, and an event history expressed as text, a model predicts the complete final grid. Reference execution makes every predicted value checkable, supporting comparisons across spatial operations, history lengths, and workloads. Evaluating six LLMs shows that complete-state accuracy falls sharply as histories grow, with substantial divergence among models that perform similarly on single events. Models with similar exact-match accuracy can nevertheless differ substantially in the number of incorrect cells on shared instances. Even over long histories, some models recover many correct changes beyond what copying the initial state would yield. Grid-Bench makes both complete recovery and these differences in returned states directly measurable across a range of spatial conditions.</p></details> | <details><summary>39 pa...</summary><p>39 pages, 15 figures, 15 tables, including appendices. Project website: https://grid-bench.github.io/ ; Code: https://github.com/grid-bench/Grid-Bench ; Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/xzx34/Grid-Bench</p></details> |
| **[AI Consilium: a Benchmark of LLM Clinical Multidisciplinary Second Opinions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22298v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22298v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can generate differential diagnoses for complex cases, but a useful second opinion must also be factually grounded, clinically coherent, actionable, and safe. We developed a multidimensional benchmark using 300 diagnostically resolved NEJM--MedQA cases: 110 for physician reference ratings and Judge calibration and 190 for held-out evaluation. Eight single-model pipelines generated independent role-specific opinions and interactive-consilium reports under free-form and structured clinical-reasoning conditions. A physician-calibrated LLM Judge evaluated 3,040 final reports and the corresponding structured primary-role outputs. Model identity separated report quality more strongly than response format. Structured final reports had higher overall mean scores in seven of eight model pipelines, with their clearest advantage in management (2.740 vs. 2.436). Compared with the structured pre-consilium primary-role opinion, the complete structured synthesis had higher pooled scores across all five shared criteria, with the largest increases in problem prioritization, factual grounding, and reasoning coherence. GPT-5.6-Sol led every evaluated component and reached consensus in the fewest review rounds. These findings show that the benchmark can distinguish model capability, the contribution of multidisciplinary synthesis, criterion-specific effects of response structure, deliberation dynamics, and inference requirements within one common-case evaluation.</p></details> |  |
| **[CAT-alyzing Honesty: Continuous Adversarial Training for Transferable LLM Honesty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22292v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22292v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are becoming prevalent in scenarios where honesty is critical. However, despite alignment training, these models still exhibit dishonest behaviour, such as outputting claims that contradict some knowledge or are not supported by what is accessible in context. We introduce Continuous Adversarial Training for Consistent Honesty (CATCH), inspired by a method originally developed to robustly refuse harmful queries. CATCH trains the model towards an honest answer and away from a dishonest one while maintaining the model's capabilities. We evaluate two architectures (Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct and Qwen3-3.6-27B). We show that trained with only hundreds of targets built from self-reporting scenarios, CATCH learns an honest behaviour that robustly generalizes and transfers across distinct benchmarks. It consistently reduces dishonesty under pressure on MASK, increases confessions in the multi-agent game of Among Us, and improves the discovery rates of the model's hidden behaviours in AuditBench. We show that CATCH outperforms prompting and supervised fine-tuning in improving honesty, and it is on par with state-of-the-art honesty activation steering. However, CATCH surpasses honesty steering in maintaining utility, thanks to a novel choice of utility data.</p></details> | 9 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Librarian Agents: Training LLMs to Curate Long-Context Workspaces that Anticipate Future Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22273v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22273v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning over long document contexts remains a challenge for large language models (LLMs). Standard retrieval methods are inherently reactive, bottlenecking agents with complex, cross-document reasoning at inference time, while precomputed structures like knowledge graphs often fall short because they rely on rigid heuristics. Instead, can we allow agents to perform anticipatory directed curation: learning to proactively reorganize unstructured corpora prior to inference to efficiently serve diverse future queries. We build on a key asymmetry in modern agentic harnesses (e.g., Claude Code): while LLM agents are adept at navigating existing filesystems to solve an immediate task, they are rarely trained to construct reusable workspaces for future use. To instantiate this capability, we introduce Librarian Agents, a framework in which a builder agent transforms raw documents into a hierarchical filesystem of synthetic files and reference links, which forms a workspace tailored for a downstream answering agent. We formulate workspace construction as a Markov decision process and optimize the builder policy via reinforcement learning to directly maximize downstream answering accuracy. Empirically, Librarian Agents outperforms the strongest retrieval and precomputation baselines by 33 percentage points on BrowseComp-Plus, amortizes upfront construction costs across repeated queries, and generalizes zero-shot to unseen domains on BRIGHT with a 31-point average accuracy improvement.</p></details> |  |
| **[Not X, but When: Diagnosing a Mandarin LLM Writing Tic through Contextual Appropriateness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22249v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22249v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recurring expressions in model-written text are often treated as signatures of machine authorship, yet most of them serve legitimate functions. Whether such an expression is a tic depends on whether the model knows when it is called for. This judgment of appropriateness, central to communicative competence, remains largely unexamined for language models. We study it through the Mandarin corrective construction bushi X, ershi Y ("not X, but Y"). Drawing on Gricean pragmatics and corrective negation, we introduce a decision tree that classifies uses as substantive, optional, or defective by testing whether the context makes X a live alternative and whether rejecting X is informative. Five commercial models use the construction 2.5 to 9.2 times as densely as a human reference. Of 307 annotated uses, only 1.30% are defective while 52.8% are optional, that is, correct but unneeded, so models fall short in appropriateness far more often than in correctness. Several reward models favor the construction even over quality-matched variants, an LLM judge favors it even where the context does not license it, and both supervised fine-tuning and preference optimization tend to raise its frequency in open-weight models. We hope this work encourages the development of models attentive to the appropriateness of their expressions.</p></details> |  |
| **[WaveLLM: Wavelet-Domain LLM Entropy Modeling for Lossless Image Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22232v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22232v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Lossless image compression fundamentally relies on accurate entropy modeling, driving a recent shift toward large language model (LLM)-based methods. However, existing approaches mainly emphasize the modeling capacity of LLMs while directly serializing images into pixel or residual sequences, leaving heterogeneous signal patterns entangled within a single prediction stream. In this paper, we argue that effective entropy modeling depends not only on model capacity, but also on the predictability of the input representation. Motivated by this insight, we propose WaveLLM, a wavelet-domain LLM-based entropy model for lossless image compression. By decomposing an image into multi-level wavelet subbands, WaveLLM converts a heterogeneous prediction problem into a set of more structured and statistically homogeneous subproblems that are more amenable to autoregressive probability estimation. To bridge numerical coding symbols with the LLM's native text token space, we introduce a dedicated numerical interface featuring value embeddings and a coefficient prediction head. Furthermore, a task-adaptive prompting strategy is designed to enable a single shared model to adapt to diverse subbands within a unified framework. Built on Llama-3.2-3B with LoRA adaptation, WaveLLM achieves 2.51, 2.24, and 2.27 bpsp on Kodak, CLIC.pro, and DIV2K, respectively, outperforming the PixelCNN-based codec using the same wavelet transform by 0.43, 0.36, and 0.46 bpsp, and surpassing recent LLM-based lossless compressors on all three benchmarks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at ACM Multimedia 2026 (MM '26). 10 pages, 6 figures</p></details> |
| **[Change the Number, Change the Answer: Assessing Rating Scale Sensitivity in LLM-Based Text Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22187v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22187v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in computational social science to assign scalar scores to social and psychological constructs. However, studies use different rating scales, and whether their scores remain comparable across scale designs is understudied. To address this gap, we define and systematically investigate scale sensitivity, the variation in LLM-generated scores caused by rating scale design. We distinguish numerical sensitivity, changes in normalized scores, from semantic sensitivity, changes in score interpretation. To assess both, our experimental design crosses 2 formats (continuous vs. ordinal), 4 ranges (3, 5, 11, and 21 points), and 2 signs (signed vs. unsigned) across 3 tasks (political leaning, sentiment, and toxicity), 2 prompting strategies (zero-shot and rubric-based), and 5 LLMs. We find that changing one design element shifts normalized scores by about 0.10 on average and changes the category of roughly 20% of texts. Sensitivity is greatest for toxicity, the only unipolar construct examined. Also, rubric-based prompting does not reduce sensitivity compared with zero-shot prompting. We provide practical guidance for designing more reliable LLM-based text scoring and highlight the need for robustness checks across alternative scales.</p></details> |  |
| **[Efficient KV Cache Migration for Geo-Distributed LLM Inference in Collaborative Edge Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22172v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22172v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown exceptional capabilities but rely heavily on centralized cloud GPU clusters, leading to high bandwidth costs, latency, and privacy issues. Edge computing offers a solution by deploying LLMs closer to users on edge servers, yet single edge servers often cannot meet LLMs' heavy computational demands. Collaborative Edge Computing (CEC) addresses this challenge by integrating resources of multiple geo-distributed heterogeneous edge servers, which jointly host small model shards and associated Key-Value (KV) cache for distributed inference. However, edge servers may disconnect or easily become overloaded, making it essential to migrate stateful KV cache to other servers for ensuring continuous service. Existing cloud-based KV cache management methods, such as direct cross-GPUs transmission and recomputation from input tokens are ill-suited for bandwidth-constrained and resource-limited edge environments. In this work, we propose EdgeFlow, a novel hybrid KV cache migration scheme that integrates direct KV cache transmission with activation-based recomputation, leveraging parallel computation and communication to minimize migration time. On this basis, we formulate a multi-node KV migration optimization problem and design a 2-approximation algorithm based on improved linear programming relaxation and bipartite graph techniques. Extensive experiments on a real-world distributed LLM inference platform demonstrate that EdgeFlow reduces migration time by up to 45%, significantly enhancing the robustness and efficiency of distributed LLM inference in collaborative edge computing.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ICDCS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Are You Sure Your LLM Is Sure? Repairing and Auditing Confidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22139v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22139v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Confidence scores determine whether an LLM answer is accepted, reviewed, or regenerated, yet scalar recalibration can only correct errors visible through the score itself. We introduce RADAR (Repair, Adopt, and Diagnose After Recalibration), a reliability framework that separates confidence repair from deciding whether to deploy the repair and auditing the errors that remain. Under squared loss, we decompose prediction risk into outcome noise, information hidden by the score, and mapping error; restricting repair to order-preserving maps additionally introduces a pooling gap. For repair, we propose Probe-Anchored Isotonic Calibration (PAIC), which learns a monotone map from correctness-probe predictions on unlabeled target prompts. PAIC's excess risk depends on the probe's score-conditional bias and fitting error rather than its overall accuracy. For adoption, we derive a paired procedure with finite-sample control of false improvements. For auditing, we develop a prompt-clustered kernel test that detects feature-aligned residual errors while accounting for dependent answers from the same prompt. Across multiple LLM checkpoints, benchmarks, and distribution shifts, richer predictors can improve confidence estimation, but projecting their information back to a scalar score can lose these gains, and lower prediction risk need not yield better review decisions. Across 47,400 replayed raw-score allocations, paired betting adopts candidates with lower held-out test risk in 42.95% of allocations, versus 15.09% for one-sided empirical Bernstein. RADAR thus provides a principled workflow to repair what a scalar score can express, adopt a repair only when supported by evidence, and diagnose systematic errors that recalibration leaves behind. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.</p></details> | <details><summary>main ...</summary><p>main paper 10 pages; supplementary 44 pages; 5 figures</p></details> |
| **[Believed but Not Said: Measuring Hidden Belief Collapse in Multi-Agent LLM Discussions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22138v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22138v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM systems increasingly rely on groups of agents that discuss, critique, and check one another, assuming that peers will catch individual errors. We test this assumption against a minimal adversary. In a ten-agent factual discussion, one or three adversarial agents repeatedly assert a plausible false answer. After each round we probe each benign agent's private answer through a separate call whose response is hidden from its peers, and we separately label its public stance toward the claim. Across six models and three factual-QA datasets, groups remain stable without an attacker: only 0.6% of benign agents finish with the false answer. A single adversarial agent increases this rate to 44.8% and three to 60.5%. Studies of conformity typically report public agreement without private change; we find the reverse. With three adversarial agents present, benign agents hold the false answer in 51.6% of exposed turns but publicly endorse it in only 19.7%. Much of the influence therefore remains invisible in the discussion, and the false answers persist when agents are asked for their own views. Agents that retain the true answer often fail to defend it; once adopted, the false answer spreads among benign agents, and baseline knowledge does not predict robustness.</p></details> | 14 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Autonomous OSS Threat Detection via Taxonomy-Aligned LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01263v2)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.01263v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-source software (OSS) ecosystems face escalating supply chain threats, including typosquatting, dependency confusion, Trojan Source obfuscation, malicious build injection, and CI/CD pipeline poisoning. Sonatype documented over 1.2 million cumulative malicious packages across major registries, while signature-based and rule-based detection approaches lack the semantic understanding needed to generalize across evolving attacks. This paper proposes a taxonomy-aligned large language model framework for automated detection and multi-class classification of OSS supply chain threats. We introduce an AV-xxx taxonomy covering five semantically distinct attack categories and curate 999 verified real-world incidents from GitHub Security Advisories, CISA alerts, government security databases, and commercial threat intelligence reports, spanning 2018 to 2026 across eight package ecosystems. Using taxonomy-aligned prompting with GPT-4, the framework achieves 97% accuracy and 97% macro F1 across all five categories. Evaluation against eight methods five traditional machine learning baselines, one zero-shot open-source LLM, and two fine-tuned neural models reveals that fine-tuned Llama 3.1 8B (70.5%) and SecRoBERTa (77.5%) underperform a simple TF-IDF classifier (82.3%), while Mistral 7B without taxonomy alignment achieves 65.7%. These findings highlight taxonomy-aligned prompting over model scale, domain pretraining, or fine-tuning as the critical performance factor. Cross-ecosystem analysis shows varying classification difficulty, with npm achieving 65.5% baseline accuracy versus 100% in smaller ecosystems. GitHub Actions exhibits a substantial accuracy F1 divergence (82.4% accuracy, 0.367 macro F1), indicating systematic class imbalance and highlighting ecosystems where LLM-based contextual reasoning offers the greatest benefit. Our dataset, scripts, and code are publicly available in Github.</p></details> |  |
| **[Explaining the Roles of Experts in Mixture-of-Experts LLMs for Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22127v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22127v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Personalization of Large Language Models (LLMs) is increasingly important for user-centric applications, yet prior methods mainly adapt dense LLMs, whose polysemantic neurons, each encoding multiple unrelated concepts, hinder localizing the components behind personalization, and thus understanding and controlling it. We propose to personalize Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs, which sparsely route each token to a few specialized experts, reducing polysemanticity and enabling such localization. We adapt three open-weight MoE LLMs on a large-scale personalization dataset and train crosscoders on paired activations of the personalized and non-personalized models to learn interpretable personalized latents, from which we identify the experts encoding distinct personalization features. The personalized MoE LLMs perform competitively with dense LLMs while activating only a fraction of their parameters, exhibit lower polysemanticity than dense LLMs with similar active parameters, and, within the same model family, yield personalized latents that are less shared with the non-personalized model. Causal interventions show that these latents suffice to recover the personalized model's behavior and that boosting their associated experts moves the non-personalized model toward it, revealing distinct routing paths for different personalization features.</p></details> | Under review |
| **[PROBE: Learning to Audit Policy Compliance in Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22124v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22124v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern agentic systems extend language models with external tools, enabling them to complete complex tasks through multi-turn interactions. As these agents are deployed in consequential settings, they are often constrained by policies governing pricing, privacy, confirmation, safety, and other requirements. However, existing evaluation methods leave a critical gap: capability benchmarks measure what agents can accomplish, while content-safety red-teaming measures harmful generations, but neither tests whether agents comply with predefined policies during otherwise valid tool use. Such policy violations are difficult to audit because their effects may emerge only after several turns, span multiple tool calls, or surface in downstream systems. We propose PROBE (Policy Rule Observation via Behavioral Exploration), an algorithm for training LLM-based auditors that interact with deployed agents as realistic users and probe them for policy violations through multi-turn, tool-mediated dialogue. We formalize agent auditing as a two-player Markov game and propose a composite reward that balances violation discovery, behavioral plausibility, conversation progress, and policy diversity. To study training objectives and reward design, we compare GRPO, DPO, and GFlowNet-based optimization in a controlled setting. Across extensive experiments, auditors trained with PROBE uncover substantially broader and higher-yield policy violations than same-architecture zero-shot adversaries, even when the auditor is significantly smaller than the target agent. Our results show that multi-turn behavioral auditing is a distinct and necessary axis of agent evaluation, complementing capability and safety benchmarks. PROBE offers a scalable path toward automated policy auditing for real-world agentic systems and suggests a co-evolutionary loop in which auditors expose failures and agents improve from auditor feedback.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages, NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Explanation Generation for Contradiction Reconciliation with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22735v3)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2603.22735v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing NLP work commonly treats contradictions as errors to be resolved by choosing which statements to accept or discard. Yet a key aspect of human reasoning in social and professional settings is the ability to hypothesize explanations that reconcile contradictions. For example, "Cassie hates coffee" and "She buys coffee everyday" appear contradictory, but are in fact compatible if Cassie was assigned the daily chore of buying coffee for all her coworkers. Despite advances in the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs), their ability to hypothesize such explanations remains largely unexplored. To address this gap, we introduce the task of reconciliatory explanation generation, where models must generate explanations that effectively render contradictory statements compatible. We propose a novel method of reusing natural language inference (NLI) datasets, and introduce quality metrics that enable scalable automatic evaluation. Experiments with 13 LLMs in thinking and non-thinking modes reveal models' limited success in this task, with longer reasoning chains negatively associated with explanation success. As a proof of concept, we further adapt our introduced task and metrics for reinforcement-learning training, finding that trained models exhibit improved accuracy in non-thinking mode on two narrative reasoning benchmarks (True Detective and MuSR). Our findings highlight reconciliatory explanation generation as a challenge in LLM reasoning and motivate further work to investigate its potential for improving model capabilities.</p></details> | Preprint |
| **[Onboard Lightweight LLM Reasoning for Human-Aware Safe UAV Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22092v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22092v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Aerial robots operating near people must treat a pedestrian, a parked car, and a wall differently, yet conventional perception--planning stacks reduce all of them to occupied geometry---a \emph{human-aware safety gap} in which category-specific clearance is unavailable to the planner and temporary occlusions remove pedestrian-specific constraints from its map. We present a modular, fully onboard pipeline that closes this gap by turning visual observations into context-dependent safety constraints for real-time UAV navigation. A shared BLIP encoder drives scene captioning and text-conditioned segmentation, which is fused with depth to localize obstacles in 3D. Obstacle-specific risk is inferred by a lightweight LLM (Qwen-2.5-0.5B) stabilized with retrieval-augmented generation and grammar-constrained decoding that restricts its output to a planner-compatible JSON schema. The LLM reasons once per object category rather than per frame: its verdicts are cached per class, so previously seen categories are resolved without inference and new ones trigger a fresh retrieval-grounded query; detections are additionally given persistent identities and held in the map across occlusions. The perception pipeline runs at 10\,Hz on a Jetson Orin NX, while fallback LLM queries take approximately 0.8\,s and remain off the control-critical path. In Gazebo, low-altitude urban navigation among pedestrians and vehicles shows a roughly $3\times$ reduction in collision events over a geometric planner, and semantics-aware landing among moving actors raises success from 34\% to 96\%. The 224-pixel encoder input limits reliable small-object perception at range; conservative pedestrian margins and short-term persistence mitigate this limitation during navigation.</p></details> | <details><summary>HEAI ...</summary><p>HEAI -- IROS 2026 Workshop</p></details> |
| **[A large-scale pipeline for LLM-assisted corpus annotation: variation and change in the English consider construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.12306v5)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2510.12306v5) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As natural language corpora expand at an unprecedented rate, manual annotation remains a significant methodological bottleneck in corpus linguistic work. We address this challenge by presenting a scalable pipeline for automating grammatical annotation in voluminous corpora using large language models (LLMs). Unlike previous supervised and iterative approaches, our method employs a four-phase workflow: prompt engineering, pre-hoc evaluation, automated batch processing, and post-hoc validation. We demonstrate the pipeline's accessibility and effectiveness through a diachronic case study of variation in the English evaluative consider construction (consider X as/to be/Ø Y). We annotate 143,933 'consider' concordance lines from the Corpus of Historical American English (COHA) via the OpenAI API in under 60 hours, achieving 98%+ accuracy on two sophisticated annotation procedures. A Bayesian multinomial GAM fitted to 44,527 true positives of the evaluative construction reveals previously undocumented genre-specific trajectories of change, enabling us to advance new hypotheses about the relationship between register formality and competing pressures of morphosyntactic reduction and enhancement. Our results suggest that LLMs can perform a range of data preparation tasks at scale with minimal human intervention, unlocking substantive research questions previously beyond practical reach, though implementation requires attention to costs, licensing, and other ethical considerations.</p></details> |  |
| **[Documenting Trofinetide Treatment Phenotypes in Rett Syndrome with a Dual-LLM Consensus Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22062v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22062v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Objective: Trofinetide, approved in 2023 as the first Rett syndrome treatment, lacks scalable real-world monitoring because chart review is labor-intensive. We developed a dual large language model (LLM) consensus pipeline to extract 18 treatment-response phenotypes, comprising 4 adverse effects, 13 Rett clinical features, and medication refusal, from clinical notes. Materials and Methods: We analyzed 26,763 notes from 108 patients with confirmed sustained trofinetide exposure at Texas Children's Hospital. The pipeline filters notes by keyword, classifies each through two LLMs requiring consensus, and aggregates a patient-by-timepoint matrix. Architecture and prompt were selected on a diarrhea development set, then frozen and applied to the other 17 phenotypes with the phenotype definition substituted, as an out-of-sample test. The reported prompt comparison scores 183 expert-labeled cells. Results: A prompt requiring diarrhea to be active and severe performed best (84.2 $\pm$ 0.0% accuracy; F1 = 0.674), 47 percentage points above regular-expression matching ($p < 0.0001$). Expert reviewers judged six of the pipeline's calls per phenotype, three positive and three negative, and agreed with 90.2% (92/102). The 13 Rett clinical features are manifestations of the disorder itself rather than adverse effects of trofinetide. Gross and fine motor impairment, language/communication, and stereotypy were documented present in 92-100% of patients at every visit. Discussion: In-context LLM extraction captured multi-domain treatment responses. The outcome is documented presence at each visit, not a direction-of-change judgment. Conclusion: Coupling in-context LLM reasoning with a consensus architecture offers a scalable alternative to keyword and regular-expression extraction for real-world evidence, adapting to new phenotypes and medications through configuration alone.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 3 figures, 4 tables; supplementary material (22 pages, 1 figure, 11 tables) included</p></details> |
| **[SoK: Trading Agents or Market Crashers? Dissecting Robustness and Security Failures in Academic Financial LLM Trading Schemes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19705v2)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.19705v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents already trade with real capital, yet agent-security research has mostly treated them as generic agents. Trading changes the threat: a compromised agent's orders move prices that other participants act on, and an attacker who profits from a crash can trigger the shock that a non-robust agent mishandles. We present FARSIGHT (Financial Agent Robustness and Security Investigation and Global Holistic Taxonomy), a systematization that rates trading agents on robustness to market stress and on security against source manipulation, agent compromise, and agents acting as attackers. It also checks paper-level ratings against released code. To reveal this effect, we introduce a belief-rollout diagnostic that traces how an agent's anticipated world state and future decisions evolve under alternative harnesses. We further separate immediate divergence introduced by the interface from divergence that develops over longer horizons. Building on this observation, we introduce Belief-Invariant World-Modeling (BIWM), which exposes evidence hidden or compressed by harness execution and aligns the resulting belief trajectories across different harnesses. Our findings reveal that harness design can systematically shape how software agents understand and act on their environments, providing practical guidance for building and evaluating more reliable coding agents. | **[MMSearch-OPD: Easy Made Hard, Hard Made Learnable for Long-Horizon Multimodal Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22240v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22240v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal deep search is moving from short queries to long-horizon investigations that continually acquire evidence. However, training such agents remains constrained by limited challenging training questions and insufficient process feedback. Our Refine pipeline performs targeted expansion of reasoning chains derived from existing simple multimodal questions. Parameterized transformations control search depth and cross-modal evidence requirements while reusing existing image--question pairs. In typical MLLMs, a projector is employed to align visual representations with the language model. However, existing methods primarily protect the language model from forgetting while continuously updating the projector across tasks. To address this issue, we propose Compatible Routing (CART), which explicitly coordinates the adaptation of the projector and language model. Specifically, CART maintains task-specific experts for both modules and combines them through weighted mixtures. CART jointly infers their weights by considering both expert relevance and cross-module compatibility, thereby promoting coordinated adaptation between the two modules. To further control model growth, we introduce relevance-guided expert consolidation, which merges experts exhibiting similar relevance profiles across samples within each module. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CART achieves state-of-the-art performance.</p></details> |  |

| **[When to Align, When to Predict: A Phase Diagram for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11190v4)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.11190v4) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cross-modal alignment (CA) and cross-modal prediction (CP) are the dominant paradigms for multimodal representation learning, yet there is no systematic understanding of when each succeeds and when each fails, a gap that leaves practitioners, especially in scientific domains with heterogeneous instruments and multiple levels of measurement, unable to diagnose why standard methods underperform the best single modality. | **[CogDrift-4D: Hierarchical Multimodal Modeling of Temporal Behavioral Decay for MCI Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20877v1)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.20877v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Early detection of mild cognitive impairment remains a critical bottleneck in dementia care, demanding scalable tools capable of capturing subtle behavioral decay. To capture the subtle temporal changes of cognitive decline, we introduce a behavioral dataset collected across seven unscripted multimodal interactions over an 18-month horizon. This dataset formalizes continuous, multi-session interactions into a dense 4D tensor (Batch * Session * Time * Feature) that explicitly preserves concurrent local dynamics alongside long-term changes. To effectively process these complex behavioral patterns, we propose CogDrift-4D: a novel, hierarchical 4D Transformer framework. To capture the complex trajectory of cognitive decline, CogDrift-4D implements a dual-level behavioral fatigue and drift modeling architecture. At the intra-session level, the model computes localized least-squares regression slopes to quantify the acute ``fading'' of frame-level facial action units and acoustic energy. At the inter-session level, a recursive cross-attention feedback encoder acts as a cumulative fatigue accumulator, mapping the patient's continuous behavioral drift across successive sessions. To mitigate environmental noise and track failures across sessions, a localized Quality Gate dynamically filters low-integrity features before final fusion, also an early fusion that integrates semantic insights with raw physical dynamics. Evaluated on structured verbal and linguistic interactions, our architecture successfully detects MCI with a classification accuracy of 0.733 +- 0.13 and an F1-score of 0.716 +- 0.14, outperforming advanced baselines. The framework provides a mathematically rigorous, scalable, and robust foundation for home-based, real-time dementia screening.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication at the 2026 IEEE International Conference on E-health Networking, Application & Services (Healthcom)</p></details> |
| **[Multimodal Remote Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.07555v4)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2508.07555v4) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We consider a remote inference system with multiple modalities, where a multimodal machine learning (ML) model performs real-time inference using features collected from remote sensors. When sensor observations evolve dynamically over time, fresh features are critical for inference tasks. However, timely delivery of features from all modalities is often infeasible under limited network resources. To address this challenge, we formulate a multimodal scheduling problem to minimize the ML model's inference error. We model this error as a general function of the Age of Information (AoI) vector, where AoI quantifies data freshness. We cast the problem as a semi-Markov decision process (SMDP) and derive an equivalent reformulation with a reduced state set. We then show that the problem has fundamentally different chain structures in the two-modality and multi-modality cases. For the two-modality case, we prove that the optimal policy has an index-based threshold structure. For the general multi-modality case (two or more modalities), we develop an optimal switching-type policy (OPT-ST), which is computed using a multichain policy iteration algorithm (MPI). To further reduce complexity, we also develop two low-complexity policies: the round-robin-like switching-type policy (RR-ST) and the round-robin-like threshold policy (RR-Thres). Numerical results show that OPT-ST achieves the lowest inference error among all baselines, with reductions of up to 80.3%. In a five-modality case, RR-ST and RR-Thres achieve $5.2\times$ and $89.8\times$ speedups in offline computation over OPT-ST, respectively, at the cost of increasing the inference error by 23.3% and 38.6%, respectively.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted to IEEE/ACM Transactions on Networking; a preliminary version appeared in the Proceedings of the 22nd IEEE International Conference on Mobile Ad-Hoc and Smart Systems (MASS 2025)</p></details> |
| **[ReasonUni: Reasoning-Augmented Reconstruction Advances Unified Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20781v1)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.20781v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Image-to-text (I2T) understanding and text-to-image (T2I) generation are dual tasks in unified multimodal models (UMMs). We propose ReasonUni, a reasoning-augmented reconstructive post-training framework that extends this coupling beyond perception. Given an original image and an editing instruction, the UMM first reasons about what and how to edit and describes the target state, then generates the corresponding target image conditioned on this understanding output. To jointly optimize both modules, we introduce TreeGRPO, a tree-structured reinforcement learning method that samples multiple reasoning paths and candidate images per path, then aggregates visual rewards to update both modules under a unified objective. Experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate consistent gains in both understanding and generation, showing that reasoning-augmented reconstruction yields genuine mutual gains for UMMs.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages, including appendix</p></details> |
| **[Reliability-Aware Multimodal Sentiment Prediction Under Local Modality Missingness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20543v1)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.20543v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal sentiment models are usually evaluated on complete utterances, yet streams often lose a contiguous span of text, audio, or video. We fuse five RM-Net seeds with a LoRA-adapted BERT text expert, using a fusion weight that depends only on the visible fraction of content tokens. Architecture, checkpoint, and fusion choices were made on the validation split. On the held-out split, the fused model reaches a macro-F1 of 0.655 on clean inputs and 0.634 under 40\% mixed missingness; its two-view average exceeds the auxiliary-head baseline by 0.024 (95\% video-cluster bootstrap interval [0.003, 0.045]). Because this split was inspected during development, these numbers are not a blind test. For explanations, exact Shapley attributions over the eight modality subsets and position-level occlusion show that top-ranked positions reduce the predicted-class probability faster than random positions (AUC gap 0.113 on validation). | **[PrismaCoder: Pushing the Boundaries of Open Multimodal Code Intelligence through Decomposed Rubric Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20538v1)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.20538v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal code intelligence connects the symbolic world of programs with the visual world their execution creates. Interest in this capability is burgeoning, as models are increasingly asked to produce charts, webpages, animations, and semi-structured documents. Recently, proprietary models have pushed far ahead, producing interactive artifacts even from a text description or a screenshot alone. Yet their data and training remain closed, and open progress is held back by the quantity and quality of available data and by the pipelines and methods for producing it. Quality is the harder of the two, since a sample must be correct as a program, well built, and above all visually right once rendered. No existing signal covers all three. Execution often returns just a pass or fail, code similarity does not map to the visual output, and task-specific verifiers do not generalize. To push open progress further, we start from the data side with PrismaForge, a hierarchical data pipeline centered on a synthesized rubric that decomposes these factors into checkable criteria. The same rubric serves as both filter and reward: it curates the supervised corpus, then drives reinforcement learning on visual-coding tasks. Leveraging PrismaForge for data synthesis and filtering, we build PrismaCoder-2M, the most diverse open dataset for multimodal code intelligence and the largest to date. On the model side, we train PrismaCoder-9B and PrismaCoder-35B on this data and release both. They lead the open state of the art by a wide margin and rival leading proprietary systems. Our analyses further show that the proposed rubric-based pipeline improves multimodal coding performance across model backbones. We release our code and data at https://qiushisun.github.io/PrismaCoder-Home/.</p></details> | Work in progress |
| **[MultiFly: A Real-World Multimodal Aerial Dataset with Annotation-Efficient Label Transfer and Cross-Modal Semantic Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.10359v2)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.10359v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce MultiFly, a real-world, low-altitude UAV dataset for semantic perception across RGB, thermal, LiDAR, and radar modalities. | **[Reference-Efficient Multimodal Bitstream-Corrupted Video Recovery via Depth-Conditioned Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15806v2)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.15806v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recovering visually faithful content from damaged video bitstreams is important for reliable multimedia communication and storage. Existing methods exploit local residuals and non-local RGB references of the input video sequence, but both sources can become unreliable after decoding, while processing additional references increases receiver computation and memory. This paper studies multimodal bitstream-corrupted video recovery (MBSCVR) using RGB and depth information, where pre-transmission depth-based geometry aids reconstruction when the received appearance is corrupted. We propose a novel MBSCVR framework that selects and effectively utilizes a single selected non-local RGB reference per recovery window to achieve higher performance. It integrates a reliability-aware multimodal selector that combines valid appearance with depth compatibility before reference encoding. Depth-Conditioned Reference Adaptation (DCRA) introduces selected RGB evidence into feature completion and content generation, while frame-aligned depth conditions completion queries. On the complete BSCV benchmark with oracle masks, MBSCVR uses one selected reference to achieve 32.30 dB on YouTube-VOS and 28.93 dB on DAVIS, surpassing full-reference B2SCVR by 0.27 and 0.33 dB and improving SSIM on both subsets. These results demonstrate how compact geometric guidance and adapted appearance processing improve the quality--resource trade-off of multimedia recovery.</p></details> | 11 pages, 4 figures |
| **[Learning Navigation from Multimodal Demonstrations with Geometric Candidate Modes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20241v1)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.20241v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Offline navigation demonstrations are multimodal, because one observation often allows several valid actions, such as passing an obstacle on either side. A high-level policy selects a mode using the goal and its previous choice among a set of candidate modes, and a low-level policy turns it into an action. In simulation, the policy succeeds in over 95\% of seen and unseen test cases, compared with at most 43\% and 20\% for four offline learning baselines and 79\% and 67\% for a heuristic that uses the same modes without learning. The simulation-trained policy also transfers to a LimX Tron 1 bipedal robot.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages; 8 figures, under review</p></details> |
| **[Multimodal Latent Reasoning via Hierarchical Visual Cues Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05359v3)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2602.05359v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The advancement of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) has enabled impressive perception capabilities. However, their reasoning process often remains a "fast thinking" paradigm, reliant on end-to-end generation or explicit, language-centric chains of thought (CoT), which can be inefficient, verbose, and prone to hallucination. Crucially, it injectively grounds this process with hierarchical visual cues from global scene context to fine-grained regional details directly into the model's latent representations. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that test-time scaling is effective when incorporating vision knowledge, and that integrating hierarchical information significantly enhances the model's understanding of complex scenes.</p></details> |  |
| **[FACES: Fine-grained Gated Semantic Alignment for Multimodal Emotional Support Conversation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20051v1)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.20051v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In recent years, with advances in multimodal interaction technologies and the growing demand for intelligent emotional support, Multimodal Emotional Support Conversation (MESC) has emerged as an important task. To address these challenges, we propose FACES, a fine-grained gated semantic alignment framework for MESC. Specifically, FACES integrates multi-source rewards to construct multidimensional semantic and functional alignment signals, employs a non-compensatory gated fusion mechanism to reduce cross-dimension reward offset, and leverages structured Chain-of-Thought (CoT) anchors to perform token-level credit assignment. | **[ChemArgus: Automated Scientific Diagnostics for Lab-Grade Multimodal Chemistry Reasoning: A Frontier Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20012v1)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.20012v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chemistry evaluation has moved past multiple-choice probes toward open-ended expert problems, yet the benchmarks that define the current frontier still score answer endpoints alone. Such scoring conflates memorized facts with stepwise derivation, treats visual and textual inputs as interchangeable, and confounds long-horizon planning with stepwise execution. These blind spots prevent any diagnosis of where the reasoning chain breaks in a model. We present ChemArgus, a competition-level chemistry benchmark drawn from chemistry competition material, textbooks, and research papers, whose scoring replaces the answer endpoint with three measurement properties. Fine-grained scoring grades every subquestion point by point with partial credit, so a score lands at the level of the step rather than the outcome. Multimodal capability is priced on every problem containing a real image, paired with a controlled text description, so visual understanding is measured against text on identical tasks. Diagnostic attribution reads paired metrics that share one scoring unit and differ only in context construction, so a gap between two scores isolates a single cause, namely integration, propagation, or local reasoning. GPT-5.6-Sol scores 23.2% on unassisted whole-problem solving against 28.8% and 30.4% under the two sequential metrics, a gap measuring the integration burden. Through rubric partial credit and these cause-isolating score gaps, ChemArgus shows not only whether a model answers chemistry correctly but where its reasoning chain fails, so that every score it reports doubles as a diagnosis, a readout of the chain rather than a verdict, aiming to guide language models step by step on their path toward chemistry expertise, one corrected step at a time, each step priced by the rubric, point by point.</p></details> |  |
| **[Shared Sentiment Evidence from Frozen Multimodal Models for Continuous Sentiment Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19981v1)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.19981v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Continuous multimodal sentiment analysis estimates polarity and intensity from linguistic, acoustic, and visual cues that may disagree. | **[On the Limits of Representation-Based Test-Time Adaptation for Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19892v1)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.19892v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language models suffer significant accuracy drops under common corruptions. | **[HRIL: Learning Multimodal Synergy via Higher-Order Tensor Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12393v2)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.12393v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Self-supervised multimodal representation learning has achieved remarkable success across diverse domains, yet capturing synergistic information remains challenging due to the complexity of cross-modal interactions. The key observation is that synergistic information is reflected in higher-order statistical dependence among modalities, which provides a principled target for explicitly modeling joint interactions. | **[LabOPBench: A Multimodal Benchmark for Evidence-Grounded Laboratory Decision Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19780v1)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.19780v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Laboratory experiments involve more than following predefined protocols; they require adaptive decision-making as the experimental state changes. | **[Measuring Epistemic Humility in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09658v3)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2509.09658v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hallucinations in multimodal large language models (MLLMs), where a model generates content inconsistent with the input image, pose significant risks in real-world applications. | **[Thinker-WM: A Unified World Model with Shared Multimodal Representations for Embodied Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18447v2)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18447v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>World models have emerged as a promising paradigm for embodied AI, yet existing systems often remain fragmented in the modalities they model and the functions they support. | **[MMTA: Benchmarking Multimodal Temporal Analysis with Time Series, Text, and Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17856v2)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17856v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time-series analysis is increasingly studied in multimodal settings, yet most benchmarks pair temporal signals with text or with visualizations derived from the same signal. | **[Are We Really Measuring Forgetting? | **[ActICL: Post-Training for Agentic Multimodal In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19475v1)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.19475v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal in-context learning (ICL) is an important capability of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). | **[Seeing, Maintaining, and Learning: The Evolution of Multimodal Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19366v1)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.19366v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal models and agents have emerged to enable AI systems to learn from and interact with the real world more like humans by integrating information across multiple modalities. | **[Heading Hierarchy Reconstruction for Complex and Long Documents with Level-Wise Multimodal Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18238v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18238v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language models (VLMs) have been widely adopted for document parsing and optical character recognition (OCR), as they can accurately extract page-level elements. To address this limitation, we propose a VLM-based framework that incrementally reconstructs heading hierarchies with a \emph{level-wise multimodal memory}. Our framework supports flexible anchor selection, and in this work we select the most recent node at each level, allowing the VLM to observe the visual, textual, and layout patterns of headings at every level. For comprehensive evaluation, we also introduce the Complex and Long Document Heading Hierarchy Reconstruction Benchmark (CLDoc-HHRBench), comprising 250 documents and 11,212 pages with expert-verified annotations. | **[Benchmarking Multimodal Large Language Models for Zero-Shot Human-Object Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18161v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18161v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated strong zero-shot reasoning across diverse domains, but their potential for Human-Object Interaction (HOI) remains unexplored. | **[SYNPRED: A Synergistic Approach to Multimodal Learning for Clinical Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18110v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.18110v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal medical data captures complementary aspects of disease. | **[PolUR: Polarization Aware Multimodal Degradation Perception for Underwater Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17991v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17991v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recovering high-quality images from degraded underwater observations is crucial for underwater exploration. | **[MERIT: Multimodal Evidence Reasoning over Image Tables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17937v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17937v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Table reasoning with language models typically assumes access to clean textual tables. | **[Safety for Whom? Benchmarking Understanding Beyond Refusal in Egocentric Multimodal Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17728v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17728v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Egocentric multimodal assistants see through the user's eyes and guide what the user does next, so their outputs directly shape physical actions, including those that affect bystanders. | **[Beyond Forced Modality Balance: Intrinsic Information Budgets for Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17347v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2603.17347v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal models often converge to a dominant-modality solution, in which a stronger, faster-converging modality overshadows weaker ones. | **[PixSearch: Region-Grounded Retrieval for Knowledgeable Large Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.19060v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2601.19060v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Retrieval-Augmented Generation (MM-RAG) augments visual question answering (VQA) with external knowledge, but many systems rely on modular pipelines that separate visual grounding, query construction, and answer generation. | **[The Image Still Has a Say: Final-Answer Repair for Multimodal Chain-of-Thought](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17663v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17663v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Although chain-of-thought prompting has improved multimodal reasoning, it is still difficult to produce correct final answers, as the reasoning can drift away from the image. | **[IoU-PD: IoU-Aware Privileged Distillation for Visual Grounding with Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15732v4)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2607.15732v4) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual grounding with multimodal large language models is commonly formulated as autoregressive coordinate generation, where a model outputs bounding-box coordinates as text given an image and a referring-expression prompt. | **[DREAM: Dynamic Resolution Assignment For Multimodal Multi-agent Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05615v3)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.05615v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent debate (MAD) has emerged as an effective paradigm to improve the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) and is increasingly being extended to multimodal settings. | **[Merit: Making every modality earn its contribution in multimodal attention decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17507v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17507v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>More than 1.5 billion people have hearing loss. A real device will not see the brain alone; smart glasses and earbuds also track eyes, head, and scene. These behavioral signals help, as people often look at who they listen to. However, they show where a listener looks, not whom they hear. A decoder relying heavily on them fails when a listener cannot move their head or listens without looking - exactly when a brain-based device is needed. A multimodal decoder must use these signals without letting them replace the electroencephalogram (EEG). Accuracy alone cannot show whether it does. | **[Efficient Multimodal Inference through Adaptive Acquisition and Sequential Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07466v2)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.07466v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal systems often encode every available input, even when a subset suffices for prediction. | **[Sampling from multimodal distributions with warm starts: Non-asymptotic bounds for the Reweighted Annealed Leap-Point Sampler](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.17977v2)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2512.17977v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sampling from multimodal distributions is a central challenge in Bayesian inference and machine learning. | **[A team of Gemini 4 Argon agents beats Minecraft](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22319v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22319v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We placed four Gemini 4 Argon agents in a sandboxed Minecraft survival world and gave them one goal: defeat the Ender Dragon. Each agent controlled a player via an open-source library called Mineflayer and coordinated with its teammates through in-game chat and shared memory. No one assigned roles or wrote game logic. The agents planned, wrote, and debugged every program themselves. Researchers could watch the run on a live dashboard and pause or stop it at any time, but they had no cause to intervene. Starting from a randomly generated world on Easy difficulty, the team wrote 2,783 programs and defeated the dragon in 27 hours and 14 minutes, dying 605 times along the way. To our knowledge, this is the first end-to-end autonomous completion of Minecraft by AI agents, achieved using only the standard Mineflayer library without pre-scouted seeds or other game-specific tools written by humans. Winning required the agents to keep a coherent plan across thousands of dependent steps for more than a day. Complex real-world software engineering and enterprise tasks demand the same long-horizon coding and multi-agent coordination. We describe the setup, how the run unfolded, the team's strategies (dividing the work, sharing what they learned, recovering each other's items), and the limits of the result.</p></details> |  |

| **[FAOPs: Normal Forms and an Algebra for AI Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22061v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22061v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Organizations increasingly encode operational procedures as multi-step agent skills, here called Agent Operating Procedures (AOPs), that must be traceable, auditable, reproducible, access-controlled, and bounded in time and cost, requirements that regulated sectors make mandatory. Once normalized, every atomic step is a separate entity with a declared contract and can be type-checked, scope-confined, bounded, tested, traced, and audited on its own. A FAOP algebra of provably sound equalities turns refactoring and parallelization into compiler rewrites; orthogonal extensions add verified termination (+V), optimization (+O), and time and cost bounds (+B); and an architectural-level classifier (0-3) determines whether a workflow needs AI at all. | **[Global Coherence: When Every Agent Is Right and the Team Is Still Wrong - A Local-to-Global Semantic Foundation for Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02036v2)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.02036v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents can each make locally valid decisions yet jointly produce an invalid result. We call this the global coherence problem: a failure of shared state, not merely of intelligence. Our Observation-Aliasing Impossibility Theorem gives the exact boundary. | **[Price of Anarchy of First-Price Auctions in Agentic Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20587v1)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.20587v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An agentic market is a decentralized serving-time market where AI agents bid to claim each user prompt. | **[What Should a Software Team Spend on Agents? | **[What and Where in Identifying and Quantifying Conventions in Humans and AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20205v1)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.20205v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cooperative success rests in part on implicit communication. The human advantage is concentrated in a situation that no agent level learns to use: hints that point to a card other than the ones they touch. | **[How Should a Camera-First AI Agent Work?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14772v2)** | 2026-10-14 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.14772v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Camera-first agents must act on the visual world as it unfolds. | **[When Is Smaller Greener? | **[MiniCorp: The Last Mile to an AI Agent Corporation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05912v2)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.05912v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The last mile toward enterprise AGI is a company that runs itself. We discuss practical adaptations for developer portal teams, including tokenomics-aware documentation design, adoption of emerging machine-readable standards (AGENTS.md, llms.txt, skill.md, agent-permissions.json), MCP server-based feedback channels, and analytics instrumentation for AI referral traffic.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 2 figures. Extended version. A 2-page extended abstract, "Documentation Retrieval by AI Coding Agents: An Empirical Study of HTTP Behavioral Signatures", appears in AgenticDev '26 (ASE 2026 workshop), DOI: 10.1145/3843282.3844445. Please cite the ACM version</p></details> |

We instantiate VAI as an end-to-end implementation on an Ethereum layer-2 network, and evaluate its coverage on three prominent agent benchmarks, two of which we re-annotated to obtain task-level policy-compliance labels. Currently, many agents do not emit user authorizations and security-relevant information as explicit, structured data objects -- rather, they embed the data needed to verify policy violations in natural language exchanges. | **[RALI@iKAT 2026: Retrieval-aware User Profile Denoising via Personalized Conversational Dense Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17673v1)** | 2026-10-13 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17673v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern conversational information access systems continuously extract user information from conversations to build user profiles for personalized retrieval. | **[An Introduction to Contract Theory for Computer Scientists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17336v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.17336v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This survey introduces contract design to computer scientists: using outcome-based payments to incentivize effort. | **[Who is the 'I' in AI? | **[agent-risk: Risk Assessment for Changes to AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16280v1)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.16280v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Before changing a deployed AI agent (a stricter safety gate, a new model), organizations must approve the change, starting from existing logs. | **[Extending concurrent separation logic to the hardware level to verify the xv6 OS kernel on RISC-V with AI agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04043v3)** | 2026-10-12 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.04043v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>MachCSL is a framework for verifying system software, such as an OS kernel, on top of low-level semantics of a RISC-V computer, based on the Sail RISC-V semantics. Accepted at The 2nd Workshop on AIGC Safety from Dual Perspectives of Model and Content (WS06), IJCAI-ECAI 2026. | **[Agents' Last Exam](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05405v3)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2606.05405v3) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent AI systems have achieved strong results on a wide range of benchmarks, yet these gains have not translated into economically meaningful deployment across many professional domains. This paper introduces Agents' Last Exam (ALE), a benchmark designed to evaluate AI agents on long horizon, economically valuable, real world tasks with verifiable outcomes. Developed in collaboration with 250+ industry experts, ALE covers non-physical industries defined with reference to O*NET / SOC 2018 (the U.S. federal occupational taxonomy). It is organized around a task taxonomy with 55 sub fields grouped into 13 industry clusters covering 1K+ tasks. | **[The Convention Gap: Beyond Literal Content in Human-AI Cooperation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11489v4)** | 2026-10-09 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.11489v4) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cooperative AI agents are often evaluated against other AIs, yet human cooperation is thought to rely on implicit conventions: shared rules for reading meaning beyond the literal message. We propose the convention gap as a diagnostic of such implicit communication. | **[Efficient KV Cache Migration for Geo-Distributed LLM Inference in Collaborative Edge Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22172v1)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2610.22172v1) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown exceptional capabilities but rely heavily on centralized cloud GPU clusters, leading to high bandwidth costs, latency, and privacy issues. Edge computing offers a solution by deploying LLMs closer to users on edge servers, yet single edge servers often cannot meet LLMs' heavy computational demands. Collaborative Edge Computing (CEC) addresses this challenge by integrating resources of multiple geo-distributed heterogeneous edge servers, which jointly host small model shards and associated Key-Value (KV) cache for distributed inference. However, edge servers may disconnect or easily become overloaded, making it essential to migrate stateful KV cache to other servers for ensuring continuous service. Existing cloud-based KV cache management methods, such as direct cross-GPUs transmission and recomputation from input tokens are ill-suited for bandwidth-constrained and resource-limited edge environments. In this work, we propose EdgeFlow, a novel hybrid KV cache migration scheme that integrates direct KV cache transmission with activation-based recomputation, leveraging parallel computation and communication to minimize migration time. On this basis, we formulate a multi-node KV migration optimization problem and design a 2-approximation algorithm based on improved linear programming relaxation and bipartite graph techniques. Extensive experiments on a real-world distributed LLM inference platform demonstrate that EdgeFlow reduces migration time by up to 45%, significantly enhancing the robustness and efficiency of distributed LLM inference in collaborative edge computing.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ICDCS 2026</p></details> |
| **[SoK: Trading Agents or Market Crashers? Dissecting Robustness and Security Failures in Academic Financial LLM Trading Schemes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19705v2)** | 2026-10-15 | [Go](https://papers.cool/arxiv/2609.19705v2) | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents already trade with real capital, yet agent-security research has mostly treated them as generic agents. Trading changes the threat: a compromised agent's orders move prices that other participants act on, and an attacker who profits from a crash can trigger the shock that a non-robust agent mishandles. We present FARSIGHT (Financial Agent Robustness and Security Investigation and Global Holistic Taxonomy), a systematization that rates trading agents on robustness to market stress and on security against source manipulation, agent compromise, and agents acting as attackers. It also checks paper-level ratings against released code. Across 15 academic schemes, none earns full credit on any robustness metric, no paper describes a way to halt trading without waiting for LLM inference, and none combines source provenance with cross-source corroboration. In the code of the 7 open-source schemes, no release enforces a stop-loss or position limit outside the LLM, 4 lack a component that their paper or documentation describes, and 2 run model-written code on the host. The same gaps appear beyond finance. Restating FARSIGHT as a domain-independent threat model, we map them onto agents in healthcare, law, software, and crypto. We then trace how compromised agents spread harm, as worms and as cascades that need no further compromise. At published injection rates per hop, a channel read by ten agents lets one compromised agent start an outbreak. We derive 14 open problems and urge deployers to corroborate sources, keep interrupts that bypass the model, and enforce loss limits outside it.</p></details> | <details><summary>51 pa...</summary><p>51 pages, 15 figures, 19 tables, 206 references.
